Training to hold the basis through samaya, training to free the mindstream through hearing the tantra, training to draw forth excellence through expertise, training to gather the doors from every side, training to traverse the stages through empowerment, training to leave the pass behind through conduct, training to cut through decisively by the view, training to take it into experience through samādhi, training to accomplish the meaning through pith instructions, and training to gather all into a single sūtra.

Moreover, those in the gathering of the great assembly who wish to amass the greatness of qualities should also train in this path. What are they? The twenty greatnesses, the twenty displays, the twenty miracles, the twenty majesties, the twenty great splendors, and the jewel of the great crest.

Moreover, those in this gathering of the great assembly who wish to attain the twenty wondrous great powers should also train in this path. And what are they? Wishing to hurl the six great worlds; wishing to suck up and dry out the great masses of water; wishing to bind the eon of wind; wishing to emanate a hundred thousand disks of the sun; wishing to destroy the three great eons of burning; wishing to fill all of space with a single hair; wishing to emanate ten million buddhas from a single hair; wishing to fit ten million buddhas inside a single hair; wishing to draw all of saṃsāra into a single hair; wishing to pervade all of saṃsāra with a single hair; wishing to churn the great wheel of the eons long; wishing to churn it short; wishing to turn the great wheel of time, the Kālacakra, evenly; wishing to turn it in turmoil; wishing to turn the great wheel of karma continuously; wishing to cut off its continuity; wishing to reverse the wheel of existence; wishing to turn it in its forward order; wishing to cut off the continuity of the great wheel of suffering; and wishing to empty the great wheel of hell from its very depths: those who wish for these should also train in this path.

Moreover, those in the gathering of the great assembly who wish to destroy the eighty-one mountains of conceptual thought in yoga should also train in this path. They are the nine conceptions of the substance, the nine conceptions of signs, the nine conceptions of marks, the nine conceptions of conditions, the nine conceptions of action, the nine conceptions of conduct, the nine conceptions of movement, the nine conceptions of clinging, and the nine manifest conceptions.

Moreover, those in the gathering of the great assembly who know the nine doors of downfall should also train in this path. And what are they? The door of falling into the class of the great māras, the door of falling into the realm of the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas), the door of falling into the realm of the rākṣasas, the door of falling into the class of the yakṣas, the door of falling into the great Rudra, the door of falling into the great obstructors, the door of falling into the class of the Great God (Śiva), the door of falling into the class of the great wild beasts, and the door of falling into the great hells.

Moreover, gathering of the great assembly: if you wish to know this path, you should know the dharmas of the Buddha. In whatever way they may be, the dharmas of the Buddha are seen as permanent; they are also seen as annihilated. They appear as arising. They exist as objects of focus. These dharmas of the Buddha are accompanied by attachment. They are accompanied by aversion. They are accompanied by delusion. They are accompanied by pride. They are accompanied by jealousy. They are accompanied by the five deeds of immediate retribution. They are accompanied by the five that are close to them. They are accompanied by the four grave ones. They are also accompanied by the eight wrong ones.

Moreover, gathering of the great assembly: they are accompanied by killing. They are accompanied by taking what is not given. They are accompanied by sexual misconduct. They are accompanied by lying. They are accompanied by slander. They are accompanied by harsh speech. They are accompanied by idle chatter. They are accompanied by covetousness. They are accompanied by ill will. They are accompanied by wrong view.

"Therefore, gathering of the great assembly, those who wish to engage in those dharmas of the Buddha should train in this path. They should know it. They should become familiar with it. Make it become the vast expanse!" So spoke the sublime Sattvavajra.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the first chapter, on the nature of yoga.

Then the multitude of the great assembly gathered in that retinue made a wondrous prostration in the manner of yoga and addressed the sublime Sattvavajra with these words: "Great being, Lion of Speech, if we may be so bold as to put questions to you, please grant us the opportunity."

When they had made this request, the Lion of Speech declared: "Gathering of the great assembly, I grant you leave to ask whatever you wish to ask, so with full confidence, put your question."

When he had declared this, the gathering of the great assembly said, "a la la ho! Great Lion of Speech, if we wish to follow the meaning of the great nature of yoga that you have explained at length, how many doors are there for entering it? What is the method? By which path is it easy? Great being, please explain its meaning at length."

When they had made this request, the Lion of Speech declared: "Friends, excellent, most excellent! Truly excellent! These matters you have asked about are wondrous: they make the doors for entering all phenomena clear and vast. Listen attentively and with respect, and I shall explain clearly."

Then, in the midst of the retinue, with the voice of a lion, he set out to explain clearly this opening of the doors of the teachings:

"These doors of the Buddha's Dharma,
Though many in number and beyond conception,
When gathered in summary, are clear as three:
The door of entering through effort,
The door of the means of accomplishment,
And the door of the result, the final culmination.

The first, again, has three:
The door of the means of the characteristics,
The door of entry of the family of the Sage,
And the secret door of the accomplished scripture.
For each one of these doors
The Guide has taught that there are three each.

The second door also has three,
Clear as the door of realizing wisdom,
The door of growing familiar with samādhi,
And the door of fulfilling aspiration.
The last, the door of the final culmination,
Is taught as the door of the three liberations.

For the path of entry, too, it is thus:
It is clearly taught as three sublime ones:
The piṭaka of the great Guide,
The door of entry of the great Sage,
And the treasury of the great sublime one: these three.

The means of entry, too, is thus:
From means arise means: three means.
When one examines by which of them,
Meaning, letters, or samādhi, one follows,
That is the sublime means of entry.

As for following the meaning:
The meaning is the mind that follows
The essence of the nature of reality.
The occasions when mind itself is joined to phenomena
Are taught by the Guide to be tenfold.

If you ask what those ten are:
The stage when aspiration arises,
The stage of applying effort,
The stage of entering the training,
The stage when perception is transformed,
The stage of entering the great application,
The stage of feeling the taste itself,
The stage of engaging objects,
The occasion of arriving at great conception itself,
The occasion when that itself becomes confidence,
And the occasion of becoming workable: these are the ten.

On the occasion of each one
Of these ten, mind and phenomena meet,
And so an awareness, an awakening mind, arises each time.

If you ask what the awakening mind is,
Its enumeration, too, is tenfold:
The moving awakening mind itself,
Likewise the aspiring awakening mind,
The rising mind of great awakening,
Likewise the applying awakening mind,
The entering mind and the virtuous mind,
The unchanging and awareness,
The abiding and the perfect.

Because it is renowned as the awakening mind,
The Victor has taught it as ten minds.
That nature of the awakening mind,
Because it is joined on ten occasions,
Appears as a reflection before the nature of reality.
How does the nature of the reflection appear?
As a star of the merest minuteness,
Likewise as an ordinary star,
And as the great stars,
And as the form of the moon on the first day,
And on the second, third, fourth, and fifth,
The eighth and the sixteenth:
It appears in reflections of these forms,
Yet it never appears as a real thing.
Like the moon in water, it cannot be grasped.
The appearing reflection of mind
And the clear sun of the nature of reality, these two,
Like the way of object and sense faculty,
Are clear as the nature of expanse and primordial wisdom.
This is the great means for traveling
The path of the application of yoga.
When one trains to become familiar with this path,
The extremes of yogic training,
The ten points, are as follows.

Just as, relying on moist ground,
From a seed that has been sown
And has ripened into fruit,
There comes the sustenance that keeps life going,
So, relying on this samaya
That has become the basis of all the nature of reality,
Through the ripening of the harvest of siddhis,
There comes the sublime sustenance of virtue.
This is the samaya of training.
As the basis that holds every training,
Becoming familiar with it is most precious.

Just as a thoroughbred steed, its limbs
Bound by fettering chains,
When released, becomes easy and free,
And goes without hindrance anywhere,
So this thoroughbred faculty of mind,
Bound by the bonds of ignorance,
Through the door of hearing liberation
Frees its whole continuum of awareness and goes unobstructed.
This is how bound beings
Are released from the continuum of ignorance.
In the training of freeing from the continuum,
Becoming familiar is most precious.

Just as, if one climbs with diligence,
Without weariness, to the peak of a high mountain,
Reaching the heights and treading the summit,
One comes to know the lands and becomes skilled in the paths,
So, if without weariness one listens and reflects
Upon the high mountain of wisdom,
One reaches the wisdom foremost of all,
And the primordial wisdom of the learned is measureless.
Becoming skilled in the meaning
Is the training of drawing forth excellence.
In these great wisdoms
Becoming familiar is most precious.

For example, just as a bee seeking a hollow
Enters everywhere and comes out again,
Then enters for certain and stays
In whatever is its own entrance hole,
So the yogin seeking the trail of the meaning
Gathers, in harmony, whatever doors of conduct there are.
Entering the doors of view, examining and gathering them,
In order to follow the trail of the meaning of the nature of reality
In the unmistaken, certain Middle Way,
This is the extreme of the training
Of gathering the doors from every side.
Becoming familiar with it is most precious.

Just as one born in a royal lineage
Is appointed ruler as lord over many,
And through the precious wheel and other jewels
Gains mastery over all,
So one born in the lineage of yoga
Is appointed ruler as lord of the Dharma,
And through the great signs, the mudrā and the rest,
Goes step by step to the head of all.
This is the pervasion of empowerment,
The extreme of training that travels with the skill
That arises stage by stage on the grounds.
Becoming familiar with it is most precious.

Just as an elephant entering water,
Without hesitation, attachment, or hindrance,
Delighting in the enjoyment of joy,
Crosses to the far shore without turning back,
So the mind of the yogin who truly practices conduct
Enters the water of the ocean of yogic discipline,
And, delighting in training great skill,
Casts it off without attachment or hindrance.
This is the extreme of training.
The sublime training of conduct:
Becoming familiar with it is most precious.

Just as the great garuḍa soaring in the sky
Cuts across the heavens with the power of its wings,
And though gorges, ravines, and rivers are endless,
All are encompassed beneath the beat of its wings,
So the yogin soaring in the sky of sameness,
Though the doors of conduct of the vehicles are endless,
Through the meaning of great sameness
Cuts through decisively with the beating wings of the view.
This is the view that cuts through
The doubts of hope and fear;
It is looking at the meaning with the mind.
Becoming familiar with these trainings is precious.

For example, a person seeking treasure in the ocean
Succeeds by enduring hardship,
All the suffering of harm
From snakes, sea monsters, stinging insects, and the rest.
So the yogin seeking bliss,
If he takes up these hard deeds,
Destroying the foes of thought, dullness, agitation, and distraction,
Finds and attains bliss.
This is the training of taking
Sublime samādhi into experience.
To grow accustomed to these deeds of mental familiarization
Is most precious.

Just as, in transmuting darkness, poison, and substances
Into light, medicine, and gold,
A person without the pith instruction of the elixir,
Though he suffers through many great and arduous labors,
Does not succeed,
While a person who has the pith instruction,
Effortlessly and with ease,
Transmutes them so they become medicine, light, and gold,
So, in accomplishing the meaning of great yoga,
Those who lack the pith instruction
On the transformation of the basis of all,
Though they practice austerities, do not succeed.
Whoever sees this secret of the mind
Possesses the pith instruction:
Whatever supreme aim they desire,
They accomplish effortlessly and with ease.
Because whoever works to accomplish aims
Succeeds by having the pith instruction,
These trainings through which aims are accomplished
Are most precious.

For example, when a wandering traveler
Sets out for the land he wishes to reach,
Though the kinds of lands are endless,
The path he travels leads to one place alone.
So, though eighty-four thousand
Inconceivable teachings of Dharma are taught,
They are words that guide and lead;
The object of their intent is one.

This is the sublime training
Of gathering all meanings into one.
Because, for certain, there is nothing but one,
One should become familiar with these.
When one has grown familiar with this training,
The qualities of practice are endless;
Through practice one becomes skilled in the path,
And one also attains the great
And wondrous qualities of mantra.
The distinction of the great secret mantra
Is that each and every thing is to be done:
It is the conduct of blessing,
Or the conduct of the means of mastery.
For example, it is like space or the ocean,
Or like a mass of fire, or a king.
These, too, are empowered
By the very distinction of their domain, great or small.
That king, too, by distinction,
Has degrees of greater and lesser
In the realms over which he holds sway.
The way of mantra in yoga appears like that.
Entering the conduct of great means
Is entering straight on, directly.
For example, when elephants enter water,
Some fine elephants plunge in and some do not;
Through the strength or weakness of their vigor and spirit,
Some cut straight across,
Some go at a slant,
And some go only a little, as best they can.
So in yoga, within the cause itself,
Though one cuts straight to the meaning of the result,
Through distinctions of high and low in the reach of skill,
Things appear according to each one's capacity.
Therefore the grounds, the stages, and the wisdoms,
The obscurations, the deviations, and the conceptions,
Conduct, samaya, and samādhi,
The times, the siddhis, and the culminations,
The distinctions of view, and the transformations,
Though all of them are nothing but one,
Their particulars of number and division
Are matched to the skill of the vessel.
All phenomena being one in the basis of all,
Through five kinds of transformation of the basis of all
They appear as the particular dharmas of the path:
The basis itself, the basis of all as cause;
Application, the basis of all as aspiration;
Appearance, the basis of all as object;
The unchanging basis of all;
And the basis of all that abides without seeking: these five.
Through transformation on these five occasions
One travels the distinctions of the grounds.
Moreover, these dharmas of the two
All proceed through complete yoga
And through the three that benefit beings:
The aim of virtue and the practitioner,
And what is called the vidyādhara, which is completion.
Therefore, through distinctions of skill,
If the lesser yogas, beginning with
The yoga of the beginner,
Abandon their own roots
And practice the manner of the great yogas,
Beginning with the yoga of completing great skill,
Wishing for the aim, it is like a frog performing the feats of a garuḍa:
The object it seeks is cut off.
Just as one whose station in wealth is low through karma,
Abandoning the livelihood that is his lot,
Sets his sights on the great wealth
Of a king, whose station through his means is high,
Yet does not gain it and is left without livelihood,
So these aims of the fortunate
Are hard for those of weak fortune to accomplish.
Because houses of various treasures
Have become the king's treasury,
So in all entry into the doors of conduct,
Through distinctions of outer and inner austerity,
The ranks of the nine kinds of jewels,
And the higher and lower work and means of each,
Though the place is only one,
The nine doors of the record office are each used separately.
Whoever becomes king
Is the lord who holds sway over them all.
When the nonabiding expanse of phenomena itself
Becomes the object of seeing,
Through distinctions in the skill of awareness,
Though the object seen is only one,
Each practices the doors of entry from every side.
In the single nature of the expanse,
Through distinctions of high and low reach of skill,
Like counting the rungs of a ladder,
In the distinctions of the grounds and vehicles
They appear in stages, high and low.
In the single basis that has become equal,
Through meanings that accord and do not accord,
Like a house with many doors,
The separate doors of the grounds and vehicles
Appear separately, not in accord.
Though one has not trained in entering and the rest
Of the doors of the separate vehicles,
Because the root of all is cut through here,
It is one, and there is no difference at all.
That is like mountain and Meru,
Or wanderer and youth:
The distinctions in what is seen, heard, and taught
Exist in that way only in meaning.
Therefore, since this mind
Is the root of all phenomena,
Illustrated by the example of space,
One should know the root of all.
The yogin who knows it in this way
Is skilled in the means of the path:
Having lost the path, sought the path, and found the path,
He finds and accomplishes the easy short path.
If you ask what the path he finds is,
It is the ground of yogic practice.
Because the ten grounds are traveled in sequence,
I shall explain the distinctions of the grounds.
The grounds where one holds one's place and practices
Are complete as a single set of ten.
On this ground where the power of change is not yet settled,
There are five yogas: contrived and mixed conduct,
Holding to the signs of a manner, devotion,
And aspiration for virtue.
With entering, turning back, and falling,
One circles about and is not settled.
This is called the ground where
The yogin of aspiration and intention abides and practices.
On this ground of the supporting basis
Are the yoga of the beginner,
Training, purification, and certainty,
Practiced with purity and impurity.
It is explained as the ground where
The beginner of attending on command and of action abides and practices.
This ground of crucial conduct
Is the ground where abides and practices
The yoga of distinguishing the great families itself:
All virtuous and nonvirtuous actions,
Through cutting, purifying, and applying,
It makes into the great power of good and ill.
On this ground of continuous training,
The heats of moving, attaining, and stability,
And likewise the great signs of moving,
Of attaining, and of stability:
These six kinds of yoga
Bring the ten points of training to culmination.
Like the waxing moon,
The wisdom of training increases;
One is not afraid of the meaning, and wonder arises.
Through that knowing in which the investigating mind engages,
One enters the solitude where the lower realms are not returned to,
Though obstacles also arise for him.
The three yogas, of the secret door of scripture,
Of continuous conduct,
And of marks and signs,
Are explained as the ground where they abide and practice.
On this ground of the treasure of merit
Is the yoga of continuous conduct itself:
The place of pure devotion and aspiration,
The ground that is the resting place of the skilled mind,
Mastering the ten extremes of training altogether.
On this ground of advancing distinctively through reliance,
There is the yoga of reaching the great breath,
Holding the great families, and great equal perfection,
True conduct, and training in great skill,
Entering objects, and joining with awareness:
These seven proceed to completion.
These ten, the causes and results of training,
Are applied in the manner of familiarization.
In rest, everything is seen.
On the irreversible ground, there is nothing to familiarize.
Though the application of these has one basis,
They are counted as individual, uncommon
Obscurations and actions,
And for each a single wisdom dawns.

Therefore, in the single extreme of training,
The Great Perfections, the irreversible and the rest,
As each is placed in sameness,
For each a single wisdom dawns,
And the last is complete through self-arising itself.
For those who have culminated, those who have attained,
And the irreversible, in sequence,
In dreams, in echoes,
And with certainty in direct perception, as befits each,
The yogin admitted to the vajra ranks
Is proclaimed "Vajra, Vajra So-and-so"
To himself, through the lands of the earth,
And through all the buddha fields,
And he teaches the excellent scripture of yoga.
On this ground where the result is born from the focus
Are three yogas: the yoga of accomplishment, great yoga,
And the yoga of definite emergence.
Born a victor from the heart of the victors,
The heir of the victors who carries on the lineage,
Complete in every distinction of the reach of skill,
It is the application of samādhi.
On this ground that does not change through abiding,
The gathering of the great accumulations, the completion of great skill,
Should be known as completing in three:
The great mudrā is illusion.
The great king who turns the wheel,
From the place of seeing, the sixteenth,
Is fulfilled in the place of perfection.
On this ground of the nature of reality spread out,
The yoga of the ornament of play,
And great bliss, and completion in the bindu:
In this completion as three,
By coming to know the joining
Of that wisdom with the meaning of objects, all is complete.
In the conduct where even illusion is not illusion,
This ground of perfection that masters all,
From the first to the last of the ten,
Is the summit of all final completion.
In the vast expanse, the essence of space,
A hundred thousand cloudless suns spread out.
Whatever appears is nothing whatsoever;
In the essence that is neither born nor ceasing,
However one looks, all things appear there;
It is cut through by the waves of the general seal of the supreme vehicle.
That, too, through distinctions of view,
Is the yoga of the great self,
Spontaneous presence, and the vast expanse realized:
Three, complete, self-arisen, spread out.
This is the path of yogic practice;
Yogins should travel it.

Moreover, yogins of the assembly,
If you wish to seek the trail of the meaning,
Do not, from hearing merely a few words,
Give rise to the notion that it is enough.
You are all yogins, it is true,
But you are not joined in yoga with the true meaning.
You transform your conduct, it is true,
But it has never become the true meaning.
You cling to acts of attachment and aversion, it is true,
But you never cling to the sublime meaning.
You enjoy vast pleasures, it is true,
But you do not taste the flavor of the meaning of the nature of reality.
You say "selfless, free of sides," it is true,
But you are never free of the five afflictions.
You crush concepts through yogic discipline, it is true,
But you never crush the concepts of subject and object.
Therefore, yogins of the assembly,
If, by the lamp of the words you have heard,
You seek the gold of the true meaning,
Then catch this monkey, the wish to realize,
Set the cat upon the thief,
Block every crack and window,
And tear down the empty house to rubble;
When you open the king's treasury,
These are buddhas forever.
If you seek the path of the true meaning,
Abandon the five faults of thought.
The mountain of coarse thought,
Signs, movement, feeling,
And sudden awareness: clear away these five faults.
Thought, moreover, is twofold:
It appears as coarse and subtle.
Likewise signs are two, large and small;
Movement is two, long and short;
Likewise feeling is clear or unclear;
And sudden awareness also appears as two:
Swift and slow.
These are the great hindrances of great thought
That obscure the meaning.
Why? Because one does not see that meaning:
Obscured as if by darkness, by overcast skies,
By dense cover, by banks of cloud,
And by mist, the true is not clear and is not seen.
These, too, are each divided in two:
Darkness and great pitch-black darkness;
Being covered over completely;
Being utterly overcast and obscured;
Great and small cover;
Great clouds massing, and haze;
Filled with mist, and drizzle.
Through these one falls into the three bad realms and the human realm,
The abodes of the gods of the four of the desire realm,
The form realm and the formless realm,
And the abodes of the śrāvakas and pratyekabuddhas.
The great fault that makes one fall
Is the six kinds of distraction by signs:
Distraction toward the nature of objects,
Likewise outer distraction,
Distraction by illness and by signs,
Mental engagement, and taking up negative tendencies.
These should be thoroughly cleared away.
Once they are cleared away, there is great equipoise on the meaning.
The faults that obscure the meaning of sameness
Arise in five kinds, namely:
Distraction through the force of sudden awareness,
Cover through the force of dullness and torpor,
Cessation through the force of grasping and attachment,
Conceptualizing through the force of rigid fixation,
And nonexistence through the force of vacant slackness.
These are the faults of sameness.

The teaching that mends those great faults,
This counsel for the mind, was taught by the Victor.
When a yogin of the Great Assembly
Seeks the nonconceptual meaning,
He should take to a solitary, secluded place,
And with a mind endowed with the three valid cognitions
Should track down conceptual thought:
Whether it exists or not, from where did it first arise?
On examination, thought arises from appearances.
If one fully knows thought to be illusion,
The mountain of thought will subside.
Thereby one does not fall into the three lower realms.
If one asks from what thought arises,
It arises from phenomena of signs.
If one fully knows signs to be illusion,
The phenomena of signs will subside.
Thereby one is not in the lowest level of the desire realm.
If one asks from what signs arise,
They arise from the stirrings of mindfulness.
If one comes to know mindfulness as a fault,
One clears away the faults of stirring.
By that power one is not in the second level of the desire realm.
If one asks from what stirring arises,
It arises from feelings.
They are pure, and there is nothing to be produced.
If one comes to know feeling as illusion,
One clears away faulty feeling,
And will not fall into the third level of the desire realm.
If one asks from what feeling arises,
It arises from sudden awareness, outer and inner.
It is pure, and there is nothing to be produced.
If one comes to know that very thing as a fault,
Then by the power of knowing it thus
One does not fall into the fourth level of the desire realm.
If one asks from what sudden awareness arises,
It arises from the phenomena of mind.
If one fully knows mind itself to be illusion,
The phenomena of mind will subside.
The aggregates are like reflections,
And mind itself is like a mirage,
Free from both extremes of existence and nonexistence.
The yogin who knows it thus
Will not fall into the fifth level of the desire realm.
By not viewing mind itself as an existent thing
Or as something called nonexistent,
One will not fall among the non-Buddhists (tīrthikas).
By not becoming dull and torpid,
One will not fall into the state without perception.
By not following after perceptions,
One does not circle in the abodes of sentient beings.
By growing familiar with the meaning of the universal ground,
One does not fall into the state of the śrāvakas.
By not making it empty and at peace,
One does not fall into the view of the pratyekabuddhas.
By not making it purely empty,
One does not fall into the conduct of awakening.
By not dividing the meaning of sameness and emptiness
Into separate, distinct things,
One does not fall into the sequence of the grounds,
And there is no gradual training and ascending.
The nature of the genuine meaning
Abides in nothing and takes up nothing;
It sees nothing at all, and there is no hearing;
It does nothing at all and is free of doing.
Of anything heard there is not even an atom.
That which does not appear as anything at all
Is the supreme appearance.
The nature of reality, which is inconceivable,
Being inconceivable, should be left just as it is.
In the nature of reality, which lacks intrinsic nature,
With the mind, instant by instant,
Unwavering, one should be continuously mindful.
When one rests thus in equipoise on the meaning,
The experiences of the signs arise in this way:
Warmth, signs, and miracles,
Higher perceptions and wisdom arise.
Warmth stirs and is attained;
Signs arise and become stable;
Miracles arise and are perfected;
All subtle awareness becomes clear.
Thereby all aims are fulfilled.
It is like darkness being cleared away,
Light arising, the sky,
Seeing everywhere, and everything clear.
By the power of knowing thus,
All the darkness of habitual tendencies is dispelled.
Just as the wind sweeps away the clouds,
By a samādhi of a single instant
The heaps of obscurations are swept away.
Just as when a lamp dispels the darkness,
The darkness itself becomes light,
So by knowing the meaning in an instant
The darkness amassed from before is dispelled.
The person who knows in this way
Opens the door of awareness onto the meaning,
And since he is endowed with the Dharma eye,
He composes what he sees and hears into verses of four lines.
Being one who has reached the ground, he engages in the meaning.
If one is not endowed with the Dharma eye,
Then however much one speaks of the meaning in conventional terms
With words that are endless and unbounded,
One cannot gauge what is mistaken and what is not.
One whose mind chases after words is a crude boor,
One still on the grounds and paths of training.
However many hundreds of thousands of words and meanings such a one may utter,
Do not teach them to others!
If he teaches, he is a thief of the vajra word,
And he will fall into the Vajra Hell.
Those hypocrites
Whose minds have not opened onto the meaning,
Who, led along by words and letters,
Teach the Dharma while not knowing it:
Teaching the Dharma without knowing
Is graver than taking the lives of all sentient beings.
Therefore, if one knows how to enter this meaning of sameness
All at once, in accord with it,
One is not left in the thoughts of whether the time of meditation is long or short,
Whether one has residual karma or not,
Or when one will succeed.
If one rests in equipoise on the meaning of sameness,
Awareness dawns immediately.
If one does not waver from the meaning of sameness,
One does not fall into the abodes of the form realm.
If one is not attached to sameness itself,
One will not fall into the formless realm.
Because sameness itself has become manifest,
One does not fall into the state of the śrāvakas.
When one sees the meaning of nothing whatsoever,
That is called a buddha.
Therefore the yogin
Should meditate on this special samādhi until it is familiar.
All is nonconceptual and unborn,
The expanse of space that has abandoned the extremes of thought,
Unmoving just as it is, sameness itself,
Beyond the domain of conceptions of signs.
From the objectless nature of space,
The mind of awakening of great compassion
Dawns as a disk of a thousand suns,
Pervading all the phenomena of signs.
From the heart of the blazing sun,
Rays of light shine clear as the supreme of syllables,
The syllable a, from which all arises.
From its nature, which can turn into anything at all,
All the miracles of emanation arise.
Moreover, from the distinctive features of its dynamic energy,
The domain of the breath, feeling,
Hearing, seeing, and the rest,
The domain of mind, the expanse of space,
Throughout to the limits of the expanse of phenomena,
Natural syllables, transformed syllables,
And limb syllables, each one,
With root and so forth, sharing a single ground;
The measure of their radiating and gathering,
And the number of the clusters of mudrās, are likewise.
This is the order of entry for the beginner,
By which one gradually grows acquainted and habituated.

By working from the wisdom of the syllables,
One accomplishes aims and generates mudrās:
Pressing down the ocean, a ladder to the sky,
Binding the wind, turning back rivers,
Quenching the eon of burning,
Riding the sun and moon, hurling the world,
Entering into the earth, lifting up Mount Meru,
The way of placing the trichiliocosm in a mustard seed,
Destroying and raising up, and so forth.
These things, which thought cannot encompass,
Are nothing but the training of the great power of yoga
That becomes workable through habituation.
Recitation brought to its end and accomplished,
Binding brought to full perfection,
Intention made workable,
The meaning seen and words accomplished,
Activity made fully pure:
One single meaning appears distinctly according to its object.
Therefore, although the special samādhi
Is meditated on the single expanse itself,
Through differences in the power of yoga
The objects of thought are various.
The yogins abiding on the first ground,
The yogin of the activity of undertaking,
The yoga of aspiration and intention,
And so on, the six kinds of yoga:
Because the reach of their power of wisdom is narrow,
Their power of samādhi is also small,
And they do not come to see the meaning.
When they meditate on the deity of their own inclination,
Whatever the seed of that deity may be,
They distinguish it by its color,
And make it move with the breath of the nose.
As far as the range of the breath extends,
From visualizing it clearly, twined in pairs and radiating outward,
They should generate it in the place of radiating and gathering.
The extent of the deity's body, matched to their own size,
Is likewise just that much.
The yogins abiding on the second ground,
The yogin of the activity of attending on command,
And so on, the four kinds of yoga:
The particular feature of their power of samādhi
Is the seed, the natural syllable,
Together with its blazing rays of light,
As far as the range where scent arises,
Generated clearly radiating and gathering.
The extent of the deity's body, twice their own size,
Is likewise just that much.
The yogins abiding on the third ground,
The yoga of the command of scripture,
And so on, the three kinds of yoga,
Hold their samādhi of focus to be as follows.
Whatever the seed of the deity may be,
They transform it into the natural syllable,
And with its blazing rays of light,
As far as the range of hearing,
They make it radiate and gather clearly.
The extent of the deity's body, about four times their own size,
Is likewise just that much.
Likewise, following the sequence of the yogas,
Whatever the particular deity may be,
It is done following the sequence of the syllables,
And the measure of radiating and gathering is likewise.
When they have applied these in sequence
And the radiating and gathering has become clear,
As far as the mind is capable,
The particular extent of the body as well:
Meditate that the extent of the body fills everything
To the very limits of the expanse of phenomena.
Furthermore, when the yogins of the Assembly
Gauge the meaning by following the syllables,
The syllables will reveal the meanings.
Why does the meaning itself appear as syllables?
From the expanse arises a, and from a
Arises the assembly of syllables, radiating in variety.
a is the natural syllable.
Since it is free of any support, it has no cause or condition.
Since it pervades all, it is like space.
a is the miraculous display of the nature of reality.
Since the miracle syllable a arises
From the state of the profound nature of reality,
a symbolizes the nature of reality.
From a the assembly of syllables radiates,
And the teachings in assemblies of words resound as an ocean.
Taking up every sound and utterance of the syllables,
It transforms them into the speech of various syllables.
The many limb syllables
Gather into the state of the natural a.
Since a arises from the vast expanse of the enlightened mind,
From the radiating and gathering of the rays of a,
Dawning in the secret place of the throat,
The sound of speech radiates as five syllables:
a ka kha ga gha ṅa.
Then the rays of a
Radiate to the lotus of the palate of the tongue.
From their coiling at the base of the tongue,
The rays dawn as five syllables:
ca cha ja jha ña.
Then the rays of a
Radiate to the tip of the tongue,
And from there the rays dawn as ten syllables:
ṭa ṭha ḍa ḍha ṇa ta tha da dha na.
Then the rays of a
Radiate through the door of the mouth,
And from there dawn as five miracle syllables:
ba pa ba bha ma.
Then the rays of a
Radiate to the outer domain,
And from there the various miracle syllables radiate:
ya va ra la śa ṣa sa ha kṣa i ī u ū e ai o au aṃ aḥ.
Since miraculous light radiates from a,
The garland of syllables arises as an assembly,
And in the wisdom of the limb syllables
The seeds of the bodies of the buddhas arise.
Immeasurable Victors,
Hosts of emanations of body, speech, and mind,
Appear beautiful in various mudrās.
Therefore the nature of the syllables
Is explained to be the very body of the Victors.
a is the seed of the enlightened mind.
The first five, ka and the rest,
Are the nature of the secret place of the throat.
The second five, ca and the rest,
Are the nature of the palate and the tongue.
The third, the ten beginning with ṭa,
Are the nature of the tip of the tongue.
The fourth, the five beginning with pa,
Are explained to arise from the lips.
The fifth, the syllables of the two sets of ten
Beginning with ya,
Are fully explained to radiate from the body.
From the rays of the wheels on the feet
Comes the syllable ha, which burns away obscurations.
From the rays of the secret space
Comes the syllable kṣa, from which miracles arise.
From the navel, the midpoint of the body,
Comes the syllable hva, from which desires are fulfilled.
From the śrīvatsa coiled at the heart
Comes the syllable śa, which purifies signs.
From the rays of the wheels on the hands
Comes the syllable śu, which displays miracles.
From the shoulders of the Great Being
Comes the syllable la, which destroys conceptual thought.
From the hips of the Great Being
Comes the syllable ra, which burns away attachment.
From the flanks of the Great Being
Comes the syllable va, which dispels delusion.
From the thighs of the Great Being
Comes the syllable ya, which suppresses saṃsāra.
From the two eyes of the Great Being
Dawn the two natural syllables i.
From the two ears of the Great Being
Dawn the two natural syllables u.
From the two nostrils of the Great Being
Dawn the two natural syllables e.
From the two rows of teeth of the Great Being
Dawn the two natural syllables o.
From the crown of the head of the Great Being
The seed of the Victors dawns as aṃ.
Therefore all the limb syllables
Are the body, speech, and mind of the ocean of Victors.
oṃ is the nature of the body,
And āḥ is the characteristic of the mind.
All the many garlands of syllables
Are the nature of speech, the utterance of speech.

āḥ is the nature of the dharmakāya;
aṃ is the body of perfect enjoyment (sambhogakāya);
The garlands of blazing syllables
Are explained to be the emanation body that guides beings.
a is the expanse of phenomena;
oṃ is the wisdom of awareness;
The garlands of blazing syllables
Are the nature of phenomena and of the nature of reality.
a is the cause of expression;
oṃ is the cause from which assemblies of words arise.
The assembly of words is itself the syllables;
Long strings of syllables are the treasury of words.
Because the syllables blaze and radiate rays of light,
Dharma and what is not Dharma
Open the faculty of speech
And unfold into meanings and words.
The assembly gathered from every sound and utterance
Is complete within the state of the garland of syllables.
All words and syllables, every one,
Are complete within the state of the nature of oṃ.
All the miracles of oṃ, every one,
Subside into the expanse of the nature of a.
a is the treasure of all syllables;
a itself dissolves into the expanse of phenomena.
Therefore the vajra of the body, speech, and mind
Of the Victors, the buddhas,
Through the particular powers of taming beings,
Turns into anything at all and appears as anything at all.
To beings who are to be tamed
By substances such as earth, space, fire, wind, and water,
It appears in the very essence
Of earth, space, and the rest, of water and of wind.
When some are to be tamed by the body,
It shows bodies, images, relics,
Sacred supports, and so on, each distinct,
Throughout all the worlds.
When the time comes to tame by speech,
It displays the various sounds and languages
Beginning with the sixty branches of melodious voice,
And the forms of letters,
And so appears in the realms of saṃsāra.
Why is this? Because the syllables
Are the Tathāgatas themselves,
They are the nature of the Tathāgatas;
And since they are the nature of compassion,
Through various kinds of skillful means
They bring all to liberation.
Likewise this assembly of syllables,
Through the various joinings and contractions of its limbs,
Makes everything understood as words.
The wisdom of the natural syllables:
I shall explain their individual meanings.
a is unborn wisdom;
It shows that all natures are empty.
ka is the wisdom of activity;
It shows that all are liberated by the path.
kha is the door of pervading;
It shows that all are liberated as beyond reference.
ga is the door of having become the meaning;
It shows that all are liberated as without object.
gha is the door of abiding;
It shows liberation as not fixed as one.
ṇa is the door of no grasping;
It shows that all are liberated, free of grasping.
ca is the door of no cessation;
By appearing as anything at all, all are liberated.
cha is the door of no passing beyond;
Since there is no separation, all are liberated.
ja is the door of what has not arisen;
By not abiding, all are liberated.
jha is the door free of turbidity;
By the stains being purified, all are liberated.
ña is the door free of conduct;
Since there is no activity, all are liberated.
ṭa is the door of no pride;
Since there is no mountain, all are liberated.
ṭha is the door of no artifice;
Since there is no doing, all are liberated.
ḍa is the door of no mixing;
Since there is no dispute, all are liberated.
dha is the door of no deception;
It liberates all illusions.
ṇa is the door free of objects;
Since there is no grasping, all are liberated.
ta is the door of no conventions;
Since there is no expression, all are liberated.
tha is the door free of support;
Since there is no abiding, all are liberated.
da is the door free of giving up;
Since there is no taking, all are liberated.
dha is the door of no intrinsic nature;
Since there are no characteristics, all are liberated.
na is the door free of objects;
Since there is no grasping, all are liberated.
pa is the door of renunciation;
Since there is no cessation, all are liberated.
pha is the door of no entity;
Since there is nothing at all, all are liberated.
ba is the door of no expression;
Since there is no expression, all are liberated.
bha is the door free of dust;
Since there is no conceptual thought, all are liberated.
ma is the door of no attachment;
Since there is no permanence, all are liberated.
ya is the door of no attachment;
Since there is no defilement, all are liberated.
va is the door of no cause and condition;
Since there is no result, all are liberated.
ra is the door free of conditions;
Since there is no discarding, all are liberated.
la is the door of no adopting;
Since there are no qualities, all are liberated.
ṣa is the door of no conceptual elaboration;
Through peace it liberates all.
ṣa is the door free of burden;
Since there is no heaviness, all are liberated.
sa is the door of the genuinely secret;
Through certainty it liberates all.
ha is the door free of cause;
Through being free of conditions it liberates.
a is the door free of the elements;
Since there are no characteristics, it liberates.
i is the door of no seeing;
Through being free of forms, all are liberated.
u is the door of no hearing;
Through being free of sound, all are liberated.
e is the door of no sensation;
Through being free of smell, it liberates.
o is the door of no sensation;
Through being free of touch, all are liberated.
aṃ is the door from which miracles arise;
Through appearing as anything at all, all are liberated.
Nirvāṇa and the phenomena of saṃsāra,
All phenomena that exist, are seen, appear, and resound,
Are fully awakened as the assembly of syllables.
There is nothing at all that has not become syllables.
In the sun of the essence of the nature of reality,
Rays of light blaze as assemblies of syllables.
In the nature of reality, which is like space,
Various syllables are finely arrayed,
And the maṇḍala of the Victors of the three times is complete.
From the rays of the blazing sun
Blazes a vajra, ablaze with light.
Because the light of the syllable hūṃ radiates,
It is called the seed of the vajra essence,
And is renowned as the field of the vajra family.
From the jewel of the sun's rays,
Because the syllable of light sva radiates,
It is called the seed of the jewel essence,
And is renowned as the field of the jewel family.
Because the sun lotus blazes and radiates,
It blazes as the miracle syllable aṃ,
And is renowned as the field of the lotus family.
Because the light of the variegated sun radiates,
And the miracle syllable ha is clear,
It is the seed of the variegated essence,
And is renowned as the field of the karma family.
The mind of all the Victors of the three times
Is vajra wisdom.
The immeasurable Victors
Emanated by that supreme wisdom
Are explained to be called the vajra family.
The wisdom of qualities is the jewel.
The immeasurable Victors
Emanated from that jewel
Are explained to be called the jewel family.
The wisdom of speech is the lotus.
The immeasurable Victors
Emanated from the lotus wisdom
Are renowned as the field of the lotus family.
Enlightened activity is various.
The immeasurable Victors
Emanated by the various wisdom
Are explained to be called the karma family.
The body is unfabricated suchness.
The immeasurable emanated Victors
In the wisdom of suchness
Are explained to be called the tathāgata family.

Therefore, the reasoning for the families is this:
The unfabricated body is suchness;
The unchanging family is the vajra family;
The source of all qualities is the jewel family;
Unattached speech is the lotus family;
Performing activities is the karma family.
All the emanations of the body
Hold the lineage of the tathāgata family.
The buddhas emanated from the enlightened mind
Hold the lineage of the vajra family.
The buddhas of the domain of qualities
Hold the lineage of the jewel family.
The buddhas emanated from speech
Hold the lineage of the lotus family.
The buddhas emanated as deeds
Hold the lineage of the activity family.
If one asks why they are called families:
In the beginning they arise from it,
In the middle they perform its activity,
And in the end they hold its lineage;
That is why they are known as families.
For example, all the sentient beings without exception
Who dwell in fire and water,
In space and in earth,
Bear the name of the very place they dwell in.
Sentient beings that dwell in fire
Are called fire pretīs;
Sentient beings that dwell in water
Are called the water monster tvarana.
If one asks why they are said to be five:
For all the Victors, the buddhas,
Body, speech, mind, qualities,
And deeds, that is, activity, make five.
As the five awarenesses and as fivefold,
All appear with complete clarity.
Therefore they are definitively explained to be five.
If one asks why: all the buddhas
Do not arise from anything
Apart from these five, nor exist anywhere else.
As for the particular names of the buddhas,
Names imputed to what has no name,
All are imputed from these five alone.
All the varieties of names, every one,
Are nothing but designations made by mere convention.
Families spoken of where there are no families
Appear as five by the power of taming beings,
But that is the provisional meaning; they have no actual basis.
Whatever appears out of nothing whatsoever
Is like a rainbow arising in the sky.
"All phenomena are like space":
So the Lord of the Supreme Secret has explained.
The equal and the unequal are equal.
There are no buddhas and there are no sentient beings;
There is no saṃsāra and there is no nature of reality;
There is no reference, and no absence of reference.
One who knows it thus is wise.
Awareness and ignorance are of one mode;
One who knows it thus is a Victor.
The nature of sentient beings is buddha itself;
The nature of buddhas is sentient beings;
Sentient beings and buddhas are one.
One who knows it thus is a child of the Victors.
For the sake of awakening, nothing is found;
Not finding anything, one does not find it.
If one sees the meaning of there being nothing to find,
That is the supreme finding.
Furthermore, if the yogins of the Assembly
Wish to seek the essence of the path,
They should illustrate the very signs of the path by examples,
And grow familiar with the meaning of the path.
With the lamp that has the eye of nonabiding,
They should become skilled in the cause of the path.
The signs of realization of habitual tendencies
Appear as various entities,
Yet they are like a mirage against the clear sky.
And if this is to be realized
Through examples that illustrate decisive reasoning:
Though there appear in the sky banks of cloud, rainbows,
Rain, and other continuous phenomena,
They are nothing but the single miraculous display of space;
They do not always appear that way.
For example, the nature of an illusion:
Though it appears as anything at all, deceiving and beguiling,
It is nothing but the appearance of an illusion's magic;
It does not always appear that way.
For example, the entity of a stone,
Even an entity that is solid and hard,
If it is ground down or made into powder
And scattered by the force of the wind,
Where then is its intrinsic nature?
For example, the nature of fire
Burns all the brushwood and kindling,
Blazing, radiating, shining clearly and spreading;
Yet if it is destroyed by the force of water,
Where then is its intrinsic nature?
For example, the entity of a substance:
If it is burnt by the force of fire,
Where then is its intrinsic nature?
Sentient beings, held by consciousness,
Though made beautiful by form and various recollections,
By sense faculties and the rest,
When the impermanent aggregates die and transmigrate,
Do not become stable entities.
Their forms break apart one by one,
And even their bones no longer appear;
Through breaking apart, rotting, and so on,
In the end nothing at all appears.
Great mountains and the like,
The worlds of stable and moving entities,
When destroyed by the three eons,
Such as the eon of burning,
Where then is their intrinsic nature?
Therefore all the phenomena of entities
Are not entities when examined,
And so one knows phenomena to lack intrinsic nature.
This is the supreme path of yoga.
Assemblies of the Assembly, grow familiar with it!
Thus Sattvavajra, the Lion of Speech, taught the path of supreme yoga.
This is the second chapter, on following after yoga, from the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness.

Then Sattvavajra, the Lion of Speech, entered into equipoise in the samādhi called Displaying Various Great Miracles. Through the blessing of that samādhi all the buddhafields shook, and the portents of the complete fulfillment of the meaning appeared. Among those gathered in that assembly, some came to dwell upon the sun and moon and to soar in the sky of the nature of reality. Some hurled Mount Meru far away. Some came to hurl the great trichiliocosm. Some came to see the true meaning of the nature of reality. Some came to be seated on lion thrones.

Then Sattvavajra arose from that samādhi of miracles. The yogins of the Great Assembly were utterly amazed, and making the yogic salutation with the whole body, they addressed Sattvavajra with these words: "Great being, what is the meaning of these vast miracles? What are they signs of? Please explain this to us in full."

When they had requested this, the great Lion of Speech declared: "Yogins of the Assembly, these are the great portents of the complete fulfillment of the meaning. When applied to these marks of portent, they are the portents that the members of the Great Assembly will advance greatly from ground to ground and will see the genuinely definitive meaning."

When he had said this, the host of yogins said: "Great being, what are the portents of attaining the grounds? What are the portents of seeing the meaning? Please explain them to us."

When they had requested this, Sattvavajra declared: "Excellent, host of the Great Assembly! I shall explain at length the matter you have asked about. Unstop your ears and listen with respect!

When the yogin who practices the meaning
Applies himself to the meaning in yoga,
Through the particular deeds he has performed,
Where his element and his ground will go
Is foretold in five ways, in summary."

When the definite portents have appeared,
The particular result, the meaning, is such
That one is able to display various miracles,
And then one attains the genuine truth.
The five kinds of definite portents
That appear at the time of the cause
Are expressed in words as follows:
The particulars of the deeds of intellectual power,
The portents of appearances and of the body,
Signs of the way wondrous substances are found,
Marks of prasena divination and dream omens,
And the portents of many aspirations.
Through these, whatever result is to be accomplished
Has its marks clearly set out in the tantra.
For example, the portent of fire
Is that the mark of smoke appears in abundance;
Wherever smoke appears, in that place
There is certainly fire.
Likewise the portents of water
Are that vapor, moisture, and living creatures appear;
There, certainly, is water.
The omens of wind are like this:
The clouds race along and break into pieces;
Thereby a great wind will certainly arise.
Just as, wherever the leaves of the vermilion plant appear,
There is a gold mine,
So by these marks of portent
Where the result will be, high or low, is known.
Having become skilled in the cause,
Apply whatever mark appears to it.
With uninterrupted diligence,
Paying no heed even if it costs one's life,
Enter the domain of yoga,
Shake out the power of the awareness of samādhi,
Rely on every antidote and train in the grounds,
And strive on the ground of yoga.
Through this one is without doubt,
In some no hesitation arises,
And entering into what is uncertain, there is no doubt.
For example, just as worldly children
Who have long been pining for their mother,
On hearing the sound that a portent has appeared,
Run out to meet her with great delight,
So through the delight that the great omen has appeared,
By striving greatly one comes to attain bliss.
Therefore, having understood the marks of portent,
It is fitting to make diligent effort.
Through the particular deeds of intellectual power,
The portents of the warmth of samādhi appear.
When the meaning of the genuine truth is taught,
One is not frightened, and joy is born.
When one gazes with certainty at the genuine meaning,
Weariness with the phenomena of saṃsāra is born.
When one is familiar with the genuine meaning,
One does not put one's faith in the Dharma of enumerations.
When one sees the genuine meaning itself,
No delight in words arises,
And one holds closely to the meaning itself.
When one sees the meaning, wisdom is born.
When wisdom has been born,
All that appears as entities
Is known to be like an illusion.
When, relying on the knowledge that it is mere illusion,
One gazes at the meaning of the nature of reality,
The very meaning that is without appearance
Is seen as without appearance.
When one sees existence as without appearance,
All the movements of miracles arise.
That itself is the portent of truth.
Then a mark appears
Like the motes in the rays of the sun;
That is the portent of stirring.
Then it appears like a mirage;
This is explained as the first warmth.
Then it appears like the waves of a lake;
This is explained as the middle warmth.
Then it also comes to arise
Without obstruction, like a great wind;
This is explained as the final warmth.
Then, as the warmth of the syllables unfolds,
It appears like a great rain falling;
That is called the warmth of attainment.
Then, like a cloud soaring in the sky,
The reach of the power of samādhi becomes high;
That is the extremely stable warmth.
Then, to the height of a single palm tree,
One's own body, without control,
Leaps into the sky and strives to rise aloft;
This is the extremely clear warmth.
Then, as though the great earth were quaking,
Everything appears to sway and flicker violently;
This should be known as the first sign.
Then, if one trains the power of samādhi,
Whatever one focuses on is accomplished just so.
If one meditates there on the sphere of fire,
It becomes the very nature of fire.
If one meditates there on the sphere of water,
It becomes the very sphere of water.
If one meditates there on the sphere of wind,
It becomes the very sphere of wind.
If one meditates there on the sphere of syllables,
Whatever syllable it is becomes clear.
Likewise, in the sphere of substances,
However one meditates, so it becomes.
That is the attainment of the great sign.
When the power of the samādhi of the intellect is perfected,
The power of truthful speech comes about.
When one sees a great consuming fire,
By saying, "May it be pacified!"
It is pacified right there.
When a harmful flood of a river occurs,
By declaring, "May it be cut off!"
It is cut off right there.
When a harmful onrush of great wind occurs,
By declaring, "May it be pacified!"
It is cut off right there.
When one sees a crowd of sick people,
By merely snapping one's fingers
At the place where they hurt,
And declaring, "May the illnesses be pacified!"
The hosts of illness are pacified.
Likewise whatever one declares with one's speech
Is accomplished, all without exception.
When the power of truthful speech is perfected,
Power arises in the warmth of the body.
Overcoming the conceptions of sleep
And attaining bodily endurance:
This is the first warmth.
Being free of food for the elements,
And one's complexion and senses becoming clear:
This is called the second warmth.
Being light like cotton,
With no comfort or discomfort of the elements arising,
And becoming free of the hosts of illness:
This is taught to be the great warmth.
Even without relying on food for the elements,
The senses and complexion are extremely clear:
This is the sign of tasting samādhi.
Further, the portents of the body:
Seated in the cross-legged posture of equipoise,
One rises about a cubit
And travels about the height of a palm tree;
This too is a sign of the great omen.
Further, in the sky of appearances
One travels on foot,
Does not sink in water, enters the earth,
Is not burnt by fire, and binds the wind;
This too is a great portent.
When the power of the truth of the body is perfected,
The power of might also arises,
And the blessing of that might is inconceivable.
If there is need, or one wishes it,
One transforms earth, stone, or sand
Into gold, silver, and the like.
One brings forth water on dry ground,
And trees spring from charcoal.
Such needs as these
Are accomplished just as one wishes.
When the might and power of conduct are perfected,
The qualities of wealth become complete:
Pleasing riches appear,
And every wished-for intention is accomplished.
One also comes to find marvelous things
That are the most excellent of substances.
These are the portents of being fit.

The portents that beings will gather around one are these:
One is honored by all, and all rejoice,
And one becomes pleasing to the minds of all.
One tames all, and over all
One becomes supreme in holding sway.
These are great portents.
Signs, and omens in divination visions (prasena) and in dreams,
Also arise most extensively;
The yogin should come to know them.
The portents that habitual tendencies are purified are these:
Constantly appearing naked,
Always taking a great bath,
Masses of dirt being cleansed away,
Crossing over rivers,
Swimming in the waters of lakes,
Being washed by streams of rain,
Others pouring water from a full vase
Over one's own body,
Being burned by a great mass of fire,
And, from very lofty peaks,
Being tossed about and scattered by the wind.
These are the signs that habitual tendencies are purified.
The portents of freedom from fear are these:
Coming back again from battle,
Escaping from illness and recovering,
Being freed from prisons,
Slipping from the executioner's hands,
Fleeing from among beasts of prey,
Recovering from poisoning,
Being released from bonds,
And ropes being cut.
These are the portents of good signs.
The portents of attaining the grounds are these:
Climbing onto thrones,
Climbing an ordinary ladder,
Climbing trees,
Going very high upward,
Climbing to the peak of a mountain,
Climbing a jeweled ladder,
Climbing stupas,
And climbing a ladder into the sky.
These are the portents of the meaning.
The portents of securing the grounds are these:
Sitting upon a high throne,
Riding an elephant,
Riding lions,
Riding a great soaring garuḍa,
Reaching the top of a stupa,
Sitting on the peak of a mountain,
Dwelling in the expanse of the sky,
Masses of cloud billowing from one's body,
Sitting upon the moon,
And sitting upon the sun.
These are the signs that the yogin abides on the grounds.
The portents of being a child of the buddhas are these:
Being entrusted with a king's command,
Being appointed lord of commands,
Holding sway over others,
Assigning the world its tasks,
Bringing the factions of others under one's power,
Being victorious in great battles,
Becoming a king oneself,
And flowers growing on one's head.
These are the signs of having become a child of the Victors.
The portents of gaining mastery over the Dharma are these:
Finding profound volumes,
Likewise many questions,
And examining them,
Explaining and teaching them to others,
Establishing the meaning itself,
Looking into mirrors,
Looking at a full crystal vase,
Examining it and drinking,
Gathering precious jewels,
A wheel falling into one's hand,
Seizing the sun and moon in one's hand,
And circling the summit of Mount Meru.
These are the portents of gaining mastery over the Dharma.
The portents of seeing the truth, too, are these:
Seeing lands one has not seen before,
Seeing the vast trichiliocosm,
Going to other continents,
Reaching the top of Mount Meru,
Seeing the vastness of the sky,
Going to the depths of the ocean,
Holding a lamp in one's hand,
Gazing into the heart of the sun,
And examining the whole of appearance and existence.
These are the great signs of gaining mastery over the Dharma.
The portents of receiving a prophecy are these:
Hearing the sounds of music,
Thunder resounding,
The roar of a lion resounding,
Hearing talk in the sound of the Dharma,
The face of an image smiling,
Hearing the Buddha's voice without seeing his body,
Goddesses singing songs,
Sounds arising in celestial palaces,
And being addressed as "Friend, friend!"
And holding discussions of the Dharma.
These are the portents that a prophecy will come.
When such signs as these,
Great omens, appear everywhere,
It is explained to be the portent that individuals who are beginners
Will become ones on the respective grounds
Of the vidyādharas.
For a beginner in yoga,
Whichever of these great omens occurs,
Clearing away all the bustle of saṃsāra,
One realizes the meaning and trains in the grounds.
Thereby one comes to touch the meaning.
Through the blessing of awareness of the meaning,
One is spontaneously accomplished as a vajra vidyādhara.
When abiding on the ground of the vidyādhara
And training great prowess on the grounds,
Various miracles of prowess arise:
Riding upon the sun and moon and traveling,
Brandishing Mount Meru by its waist,
Hurling the world far away,
Quelling the eon of burning,
And all such miracles as these
One is able to display through special prowess.
Such magical feats as these
Are the miracle of magical power.
The miracle of instruction is this:
To all beings who are to be tamed,
In their own languages and in accord with the Dharma,
One is able to teach the various doors of taming,
Each in its own language.
That is instruction.
The miracle of revealing the doors of the Dharma,
The definite one, is taught thus:
Through the special quality of the prowess of meditative absorption,
The world realms are shaken,
And wonder arises in all;
Satisfying the thoughts of their minds,
Each comes to taste the flavor of joy.
This is the miracle of revealing phenomena.
The utterance of all in every way is this:
From the garland of secret letters,
The miraculous sound of the Dharma is proclaimed,
So that all phenomena that are seen and that exist, all appearance and sound,
All resound as the sound of mantra,
And there is nothing at all that has not become mantra.
Therefore, from special prowess
All qualities and the rest arise.

Thus spoke the great Lion of Speech, Sattvavajra.

From the great king of tantras, the Vajra Array, the Sūtra of the All-Gathering Awareness, this is the third chapter, the teaching of the miracles.

Then the Lion of Speech, Sattvavajra, entered into equipoise in the meditative absorption of the supreme secret of the inviolable samaya. Then, when he had risen from that meditative absorption, the great yogins of the assembly said this: "Lion of Speech, great being, we see you yourself as the very embodiment of the great samaya."

When they had said this, the great being declared: "Yogins of the assembly, know that the life force of all phenomena is samaya!"

Then the host of the Great Assembly said this to the great being:

"Great being, we wish to hear the secrets of the supreme samaya. Having heard them, we wish to understand them. Having understood them, we wish to abide in them. So, great being, please explain them extensively."

When they had requested this, the great being declared: "Host of the Great Assembly, the samayas are these:

The samaya of utmost secrecy is this:
From those who are not vessels,
In the three ways, outer, inner, and secret,
Practicing the means of concealment,
The secret teachings are to be kept secret.
Bad vessels, unfit for the teaching,
Their kinds summed up in four types,
I explain so that yogins of the future who have the essence
May recognize them and avoid them.
Worldly people, those of the lesser vehicle,
Non-Buddhists (tīrthikas) who delight in wrong views,
The faithless, the dull-witted, the distracted and torpid,
Those not diligent in training and listening, who are like animals;
Those whose faith is reversed, whose samaya is broken, who have strayed from the meaning,
Whose rope of awakening is cut at its depth,
The root of whose mind's sprout is scorched:
They are vessels turned face down in the swamp of the lower realms.
To those who are no vessels, turned face down,
The secret is not to be spoken.
If it is spoken, one becomes turned face down oneself.
Though they speak the secret words with their mouths,
They do not know the hidden, secret meaning.
Among the flawed who broadcast it with bluster,
The secret meanings are spoken all too freely.
With the secret words of approach and accomplishment,
They listen to the sound but do not pursue the meaning.
Unable to keep the essential meaning secret,
Without essence, they leak away into saṃsāra.
Whoever speaks the secret
To a bad vessel unable to hold the secret meaning,
He and that unfit, bad vessel, the two of them,
Leak away alike into the realm of saṃsāra.
Though they know how to talk of the three sacred treasuries
Of view, samaya, and practice,
They lack the power of familiarity, and it has not become stable.
Tormented by deceit, jealousy, and avarice,
Fixing their minds on a dharma of non-action,
Coarse in conduct, their samaya torn, without restraint,
Those who say of yogic conduct, 'Its view is inferior,'
Are defeated and transmigrate in saṃsāra.
One who speaks the secret meanings
To a bad vessel transmigrating in saṃsāra,
Because the secret has thereby been impaired,
Becomes just like that broken one.
Bad companions, broken and ruined, who stir up retorts,
Boastful, double-tongued, twisting things,
Holding the five afflictions in their minds,
Delighting in theft, deceitful conduct, and lies,
For the sake of renown in this single life,
Trailing after persons for a few words,
Listening and eager to repeat them, putting on airs:
Venomous speakers of falsehood, fallen to the lowest.
To speak the secret points of focus
To such a venomous, fallen vessel
Is a great breach of yoga;
One falls to the very bottom of saṃsāra.
Thus, to a defeated bad vessel,
Whatever is the meaning and the nature
Of the secret, the great secret, and the rest,
If it is taught, it becomes an obstacle of defeat and downfall.
Therefore it is not to be taught.
If it is taught,
Those who teach and speak and those who listen,
Both will come to terror, ruined both visibly and for a long time.
Those who do not know yoga
Who, under the sway of arrogance, toward a yogin
Learned, clear, and possessed of the meaning,
Give rise to however many instances of jealous thought,
For that many eons, equal in number,
Will experience unbearable results.
If they are to take up the remainder of their karma,
Only after that many eons must they first enter in.
Even one learned, clear, and possessed of the meaning,
If he thinks ill as described above,
He too takes on a wretched state,
And that learned one cannot escape grief over it.
If one disturbs with evil words the mind
Of a yogin possessed of the meaning and in harmony with the way,
And assails him with exaggeration and denial, theft, and violence,
One falls into the Vajra Hell.
The incidental ripening, too,
Becomes like it, in keeping with its cause.
Yogins of low fortune,
Their root broken, flawed in nature,
Possessed of evil minds, wrath, and ferocity,
Engage endlessly in deeds of slander.
Those of ignorant temperament,
Childish in nature and dull-witted,
With exceedingly inferior views and minds,
Pay honor to hypocrites.
For those who have the qualities of samaya,
Who abide in love and are always patient,
Of excellent conduct, who keep straight the scriptures of the meaning,
For them they have no regard.
While practicing samaya like a monkey,
Buddhas who have attained the heart of awakening
Do not come forth;
I have truly never seen them come forth.
Toward a yogin who has become a child of the Victors,
Skilled in showing the path and clearing away faults,
Some, out of arrogance and jealousy,
Revile him by means of anger and rejection.
That is a stone sunk to the bottom of saṃsāra;
Never will it rise to the surface of liberation.
Yogins who are ignorant
Do not follow a person
Possessed of the true meaning,
But follow whoever has wealth and bustle
And bow down to him.
That is a traveler on the wrong path.
Over yoga's fall and awakening's gain,
No concern arises in them, even when the lifeline is cut;
But for material things, wealth, and the profit of guile,
However small, they will wager their lives to get it by any means.
One with the nature of a learned yogin,
Like a lamp within a vase,
Though possessing the treasure of the treasury of wisdom,
Like the claws of the creature Caurī,
Shows the meaning hidden within,
Yet fools see nothing of it at all.
For example, it is the same in this way:
A man who carries a treasure on his body
And men who have become fools dwell together;
Though they keep company, the two look alike, with no difference.
They utter praises of meditative absorption,
But do not pursue meditative absorption in earnest.
They utter praises of the authentic,
But do not pursue the authentic in earnest.
They utter praises of samaya,
But do not pursue samaya in earnest.
Just as some beings
Tell of the qualities of precious things, saying,
'A precious jewel is like this:
All desired qualities come from it,'
And other beings ask them,
'If desires come forth like that,
Do you have even a little of such a thing?'
And, having none, they reply,
'I have merely mentioned a few of the qualities
Of something that fulfills one's needs;
That itself I do not have';
Just so, yogins who are not diligent,
Lacking the teachings in which one should train,
Though they utter praises of the nature of reality,
Have no nature of reality in themselves.
The wrong conduct of those who act in this way
Should be avoided by those who hold samaya.
Those Dharma-holding yogins
Who, seeing yogins who have broken their yoga and are full of perverse craving
As enemies who destroy the secret,
Expel them and cut them off with punishment,
As one who sees an evil:
That person is a child of the Victors.
For example, when misdeeds
Come into conflict with the law, a minister
Who does not let the violation stand but brings it into line with the law,
That minister who straightens what is crooked in the command,
Becomes widely renowned everywhere
As 'the excellent keeper of the king's heart.'
If a poisonous plant grows in a garden,
As soon as its owner knows it for poison,
He pulls it out by the root and does away with it.
Why? Because if, from those garden vegetables,

The poison were to spread, one would die.
Just so, that venomous one who has broken his commitments
The yogin who keeps the discipline
Should banish, by place or for years, and shun.
Those who do not shun them but become their protectors
Are samaya-demons of the secret.
A yogin of the meaning who,
Toward the pledges that have been profoundly set,
Keeps an unwavering intent to guard them,
Even if, with regard to the root and the branches,
He should transgress without being aware of it,
Though there is a ripening, his root is not broken.
Just as the elephant Eneya,
Though it dies and becomes a corpse,
Still serves a purpose through its bezoar,
So a broken yogin, though he has become a corpse,
Is still an object honored by others
And is able to serve that purpose.
Though that is so, all such as these
Should not be seen even in a dream.
Why? Just as a little milk gone bad
Turns all the milk bad,
Through a single yogin who has broken his commitments
All who practice yoga become corrupted.
All the false yogins
Who bring victory to the battle of saṃsāra
Should all be diligently shunned
By the yogin skilled in the meaning.
If they are not shunned and they practice wrongly,
The battle of saṃsāra is won,
And the might of the blessing of truth grows weak.
All say, 'Practicing virtue is of little use,'
And grow weary of the true meaning,
And take delight in false teachings.
Because all practice wrong deeds,
Various unwelcome things arise,
And through this, both exaggeration and denial come about.
As for the punishment of the command,
By the very nature of their view, the wisdom beings
Never bring any harm at all;
But the savage, mighty ones
Who dwell in their retinue,
The moment they see the faults
Of yogins who practice wrongly, cut them off with punishment,
And all fall headlong into the lower realms.
There is one supreme vessel for practice,
Unlike these, complete in every respect:
Of gentle disposition, possessed of the meaning, of good character,
Attached to nothing, with a generous mind;
Having opened the lock of delusion on the mind
With the key of wisdom and liberation,
Possessed of the eye of the heart of the Dharma,
Seeing the meeting of permanence and annihilation, the domain of the Middle Way,
Knowing how to enter the unerring path,
A pillar, a great pillar, thoroughly tamed.
One able to keep the scriptures straight,
In accord with the pledges as they come in scripture,
Is a child of the Victors;
Therefore the door of the secret should be opened to him.
To the extent that one keeps samaya,
It is like a wish-fulfilling jewel,
From which pleasing desires and bliss come forth.
It is the mother of all the vidyādharas and Victors.
Likewise, to the extent that one keeps samaya,
It is also the support that makes one abide.
Samaya is the life force of all;
Samaya is the supreme wealth of all.
This samaya is the refuge from fear;
Samaya is the supreme path to travel;
Samaya is the boat that ferries across the river;
Samaya is the sacred accomplishment;
Samaya is the supreme friend;
Samaya is the eye of the blind.
To the extent that one keeps samaya,
It conquers the opposing factions
And brings victory in the battle of saṃsāra.
Therefore guard samaya continuously.
In brief, one's own three doors
Held to the limit of the three purities;
The deity, companions, and the master,
And oneself pure, bringing no shame;
The three modes not harmed, the three vows taken;
The four points of complete equality,
Brought to completion, with the four kinds of seals and signs;
Count and dedicate them in mindfulness of the summarized meaning.
The samaya of practicing the discipline
Comes first of all samayas:
Like the fox and the thoroughbred steed,
Free of the very names of accepting and rejecting, a lord of great wrath,
With a mind like a maddened elephant,
Heroic and bold, a supreme tiger, fierce and wild,
A great garuḍa soaring in the sky that does not look back,
A fierce, stocky great bear that stands alone,
With mind mastered and irreversible, like gold in the ocean,
With the seeking mind of one dumb and deluded,
Unchanging as a mountain, of great stability,
Like the sky, free of all wideness and narrowness,
Fierce as a tongue of meteoric iron,
A lord of the discipline of Vajrapāṇi,
Careful and cautious, with a quiet, reticent mind,
With the certain pride and bearing of a bull,
Peerless as the lion, king of beasts,
With a mind of attached companionship like the rejam bird,
Wily, a great whip, a net of illusion,
Enjoying all things without clean or unclean,
Unhindered in killing, like the jackal, wolf, and crow,
With the great pride and mind of the supreme tiger,
With the crouching, wary mind of the soaring garuḍa,
Mastering the ground, taking heed, protecting the realm.
The great discipline of yoga,
Holding twelve and three in mind,
Practiced through familiarity,
Is the secret austerity of yoga.
One who has a continuum of leisure and holds the pledges:
The guarding, the uttering, and the secrecy
Of vajra body, speech, and mind;
The samayas of practicing the branches;
Accepting what is pleasing;
Practicing the profound discipline;
Of all the yogas of yoga,
Not discarding and not abandoning, joined together;
The things one of good family should know;
And what is to be attained and accomplished.
